Before we started excavating in Perak, several sites had already been exa- 
mined* In 1880, L, Wray noted shell and bone deposits in Perak, but as they were 
not excavated, this discovery was not of use to science. In 1886 he carried out 
excavations in some rockshelters in Gunung Cheroh, near Ipoh, He found there 
deposits of shells and bones, remains of the food of the old cave-dwellers. As 
regard stone implements, he unearthed grinding-slabs, grinding-stones, pounding-stones 
and one proto-neolith, a large, flat, elongated and water-worn stone, polished at one 
end to form a cutting edge. 

He appears to have ' conducted excavations at Gunung Cheroh during several 
years and got, besides the above-mentioned stone implements, pieces of black and 
red haematite and human remains. It may be specially noted, that he describes 
two of the skeletons as lying on their sides with knees drawn up and close together. 
Although Wray, in his papers, says that he does not believe that these were real 
burials, we think he records here two true graves of a people who used the „HtJcker 
position in interring their dead. (Journ. Royal Anthrop. Inst. VoL XXVI; Joum. 
F. M. S. Museums. Vol I, Part I). 

After W^ray left the Peninstda, prehistoric researches were taken up by one 
of us (I. H. N. E.) in 1917. The caves and rock shelters explored were at Leng- 
gong in Upper Perak, (Journal F. M. S. museums. Vol. VII. Part IV). Batu 
Kurau, about twentyseven kilometres from Taiping, (Id. Vol. IX. Part 1). Gunung 
SSnum in Pahang and a complex of caves and shelters (Kota Tongkat, Kota G^langgi, 
etc.) in the same State. (Id. Vol. IX, Part 1). The last mentioned caves lie in a limestone 
hill to the north of the Benta-Kuantan road, and eighteen kilometres from J^rantut. 

The Gunung Kurau cave did not give very reliable results as the layers 
appear to have become shifted time and again by the floods of the Kurau river. 
However it produced two good implements of a middle-neolithic culture, one of them 
even belonging to a late period of this stage. 

The excavations in the Guwa Kajang, near Lenggong, were more successful. 
Among the stone implements found there were five palaeoliths, one being a very small 
but perfect Sumatra-tj^pe, and another a broken specimen of the same kind, while one 
of the flakes found seems to be a much used knife. The latter fact is remarkable, 
because stone knives are very rare in this country, the old inhabitants probably 
using bamboo as a material instead. In the upper layers, pottery was found, 
some sherds being cord-marked. 

Of the Pahang-caves, that in Gunung SSnum proved to be the most interesting 
as it produced, besides four palaeoliths chipped on both sides, two good proto-neoliths 
and part of the edge of a real neolithic implement belonging to the earlier period 
of the Upper Neolithic. The same site also gave cord-marked pottery, red and 
black haematite and grinding-stones coloured with the red paint. 

From the other above-mentioned caves in Pahang came some flakes and rude 
palaeoliths, but special attention must be drawn to the parts of highly polished axes 
of the latest neolithic period from Kota Tongkat. In the last-named cave a cunous 




186 OUDHEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG, DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 


polishing stone was also found. Its sides are channelled longitudinally and it is 
four-sided and tapers to a point. 

To end this review of previous prehistoric research in the Peninsula we may 
draw attention to the pottery found in the Kota Tongkat cave, where, in addition 
to the well-known cord-marked pottery, got also from other places, sherds of 
earthenware without ornament, and others decorated with parallel lines, came to 
light. As at Lenggong, all pottery was from the upper layers. 

After we had agreed to co-operate in attempting to get an insight into the 
spread of lithic culture from Indo- China through the Peninsula and into Sumatra, 
we started excavations at what seemed a suitable site, the Guwa Badak (Rhinoceros 
Cave) near Sumpitan in Upper Perak. 

In excavating a sector of this cave to a depth of some feet, we found chips 
and other implements in the various layers, but, on the whole, results were so poor 
that we stopped work. 

As we knew, from excavations, made by Mr. W. M. Gordon in 1921, that 
the Guwa Kfirbau in the limestone massif of Gunung Pondok, near Padang R^ngas 
(Perak) was much richer, we considered it of more importance to start digging at 
the latter site, than to keep on investigating the rather barren deposits of the 
Guwa Badak. 

However, attention must be drawn to the fact, that in the Guwa Badak, 
from the same layer and even lying quite near one another, we got cord- marked 
pottery and two pieces of a polished implement belonging to an upper-neolithic 
culture. The connection between cordmarked pottery and the Upper-Neolithic, 
established by the French for Indo-China is now proved also to have existed in 
the Peninsula by these finds at the Guwa Badak. 


To the north of the railroad, between Taiping and Kuala Kangsar, lies a 
large and very precipitous limestone mass, known as Gunung Pondok (Hut Hill). 
At the east side of this hill is a rather long, but not very broad, rock-shelter, called 
Guwa Kerbau (Buffalo's Cave) (PL 16, h 2; 17, i). At this site, in 1921, as already 
noted, Mr. W^, M. Gordon sunk two pits and obtained much interesting material. 
As the excavations appeared not to have been carried out in a very methodical 
manner, we abstain from using the finds he made, especially as he did not unearth 
objects of any importance of which we did not encounter examples ourselves. 

The examination of the whole rock-shelter would have occupied a very 
considerable time; so we determined to excavate one sector only. For this we chose 
a place in the broadest part, lying between the two pits made in 1921. The surface 
of the deposits consists of a not very broad and alm ost horizontal platform under 
the shelter, with a slight tilt from north to south, while the connection with the 
lower ground in front is by a sloping bank. 




OUDHEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG. DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 187 


For the zero-point we took a spot on the platform just in front of the most 
northern of the two pits of 1921. Our system of excavation was to take off the 
soil over the whole length and breadth of the sector in horizontal layers, each of 
which, starting with A for the topmost, we denominated by a letter of the alphabet. 
The exact places of pieces that appeared to us of great interest were measured by 
the aid of a boussole tranche-montagne. All such pieces were given numbers, and 
these appear in the plan and in the vertical sections accompanying the report. 
Other finds were only marked with the letter of the layer from which they came. 

Through the whole of the seven layers that we excavated the soil was of 
the same powdery nature, of a reddish-brown colour. This, when wet, became 
darker and rather clayey. In the upper parts of the slope, and at the foot of 
the embankment, it was much more mixed with humus. Big boulders, fallen from 
above, were first met in layer D, below point A in the maps, but, in layer E and in the 
subsequent layers, were spread through the whole of the sector. At the place where the 
platform merges into the slope, we found a bank of shells, as shown in the maps. 
Shells were encountered in all the layers of the whole sector, except in the lower 
part of the slope, but in this bank they were present in such numbers as to im- 
pede digging very considerably. Obviously people, sitting in the recess of the 
shelter, and feeding on the molluscs, had thrown the shells outside. Some of these 
remained on the outer edge of the platform, while others rolled down below. 
This fact has been very useful to us in reconstucting the old slope as it existed in 
the time of the mollusc-eaters, and it proved to have been much steeper than that 
of the present day (P/. 17, 2 )* It may be noted, furthermore, that though the cave- 
dwellers ate molluscs during the whole time that they inhabited the station, as far 
as we excavated it^ at the period when the bank was formed molluscs were a 
very large part of their diet. 

A second shell bank was encountered in the lowest layer that we excavated^ 
but, as one of us had to return to the Dutch East Indies, we discontinued work 
and conclusions about this await further investigations. 

W^e have already stated that the soil throughout the sector was of the 
same nature, and no alterations in colour or texture proved the existence of diffe- 
rent layers, of holes dug in former times, etc. The occurrence throughout the 
deposits of layers of ashes^ remains of old fires, showed, however, that no major 
disturbances of the soil had taken place. With regard to minor disturbances, 
numerous holes, made by burrowing animals, were encountered (PL 17, 2 ), How 
careful one must be in drawing conclusions while excavating in such a place is 
proved by the fact, that the butt end of a cigarette, thrown down by one of us, 
was recovered at a good depth underground, where a cricket had pulled it down. 

Incomplete human skeletons of which the bones were much damaged were 
found at the innermost part of the shelter, at the base of the limestone cliff, in 
the layers F and G, at a depth of 3.18 meters and below this. As these remains, 
together with all those obtained at previous excavations in the Peninsula, will be 


188 OUDHEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG, DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 


sent for study to a physical anthropologist in Europe we abstain from any comment 
on them. We would however draw attention to the fact that as the layers of ashes 
just above the skeletons were undisturbed, these human remains must be of a date 
certainly not later than the strata among which they were found (PL 18, i and 2 ). 

As we have remarked previously a part of the diet of the cavedwellers 
consisted of molluscs. About seventy-five per cent of the shells encountered are 
those of species of jiffelania while those of the genus Unio were also not uncommon. 
The topmost whorls of all the Jflelania shells have been stmick off, a method still 
employed by the Malays, before cooking, to make the extraction of the mollusc 
easy. Besides these fresh-water molluscs, in the four upper layers (A, B, C, D,), 
marine shells were found in fair numbers, especially those of species of Area and 
Cyrena, while a few shells of a Pinna also occurred. In layer D a squared piece 
of a shell, probably of a f^oluta, was met (FI. 28, e). \Ve consider that it was 
most likely used as an ornament, but, as it has no hole drilled in it for suspension, 
we are not sure about this. The central whorls of a nautilus shell (N. pompiliud), 
with bases of the septa still attached, was discovered in layer G. This, too, may 
well have been brought by the cave-dwellers for use as an amulet. Again there is 
no hole for suspension, but it may easily have been secured by a cord (PL 28, 3 ). 

In the lower layers marine shells became rarer and rarer, until they almost 
disappeared. Those of a species of Cycbphorud occurred in small numbers throughout 
the deposits. 

Nearly all the larger animal bones that we found have been much broken, 
as if the marrow had been taken from them. A percentage have also been blackened 
by burning. We have been able to detect remains of the following animals — all 
seemingly of species extant in the country to-day deer, wild ox, pig, porcupine, 
bamboo rat, monkeys, small carnivores, python, soft-turtle, fish. 

As already mentioned, we call all chipped stones without trace of polishing 
palaeoliths, while we have retained the name of Sumatra type for the special kind, 
chipped on one side only, and having the original surface of the pebble left on the other- 

Flakes and chips occurred throughout the deposits, but the palaeoliths proper 
were found first in layer B. These belonged to several types (PL 20, 4, s and f, 
21, 3 a-b-c, 22, 1 , 2 , 4 — s). The most remarkable specimen of all we got from the 
shell bank at a depth of 1.86 metres. It is a fine Mousterian point made from 
a big flake of biotite-homblende schist. It is chipped on one surface only, and 
has a triangular form (PL 21, 1 a-b). The most common type met was the well- 
known coup-de-poing in almond-shaped, oval and elongated forms. Scrapers both 
with rounded and indented (4 encoche) edges were not uncommon. In the lower 
layers, starting from layer E, small picks were present (PL 22, 3 ). The forms of 
these, however, are essentially the same as those of some of the proto-neoliths 
and the question as to whether a type of proto-neolith was evolved from the pick 
by polishing one end, or whether the picks are merely a stage in the manufacture 
of proto-neoliths remains unsettled. 




dUt)HEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG, DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 189 


It is curious that the pcJaeoliths proper are nearly all of black or dark 
grey rock, while, of the Sumatra types, far the greater percentage is made from 
quartz. The topmost of these was found in layer B at a depth of 1.47 metres. In 
Sumatra, with one or two exceptions, all implements of this type have the same 
form ^ a length about double the breadth, or more, and except in the case of the 
picks, both ends rounded. Of this kind several specimens also occurred at Gunung 
Pondok, among them the biggest example, found at a depth of 3.56 metres in layer 
G. (PL 19, 1 a-b-c). 

At Gunung Pondok, however, other varieties can be distinguished. First of 
all scrapers occur, nearly all more or less round and with vertical chipping at the 
edge. The best specimen, from a depth of 3,35 metres, is made from a flat pebble 
and has the natural surface on either side (PL 20, 5 a-b). The true almond and 
oval shapes of the coup-de-poing are also found (PL 2J, 1 — 9 )- A rather curious 
artefact came from a depth of 3.99 metres. The original pebble had an elongated 
shape so suitable that it was only necessary to remove some chips at one end, 
and on one surface, to form quite a useful implement. (tL 20, 2 a-b). These 
implements from Gunung Pondok are usually very small as compared with those 
from Sumatra. The smallest of all measured only 6,5 cms. Real picks, such as are 
not uncommon in Sumatra, were not found at Gunung Pondok, although some of 
our pieces, judging from their extreme thickness, may well have been used for this 
purpose. 

The first fragment of a proto-neolith came from layer D at a depth of 2.40 
metres, while a complete specimen occurred in the same layer at a depth of 2.50 
metres. They persisted to the bottom of the excavations that we made, the lowest 
being found in a pit sunk below layer G, at a depth of 5.74 metres. 

Just as in Indo-China, the proto-neoliths that we got (PL 24,2 a-b, 25, j , 2, 5, e) 
show different stages in the skill of their makers and one piece is even so far advanced 
that it may be classified with the middleneoliths of our French colleagues ( PI. 25, 
Another manufact, though still roughly shaped, had already the true form of a real 
neolithic chisel (PL 24, 1 a-b). Besides the polished edge of all proto-neoliths, this 
fragment shows an attempt at polishing the sides. Most of our proto-neoliths are 
only fragments, which makes it difficult to judge of their original form, but we think 
that they may have been modelled on the older types ot true neolithic axe (types 
II and III of Callenfels' classification of the neoliths). In the only two flattened 
proto-neoliths that we got it seems likely that their shape was governed by the 
nature of their material, a kind of laminated shale (PL 25, 3 a-b). 

The next kin ds of implements about which we wish to say something are 
the grinding and pounding stones. All of them are natural water- worn pebbles and 
unchipped. However, it is a curious fact that the greater number of them have 
grip-marks on one or both, generally both, faces (PI. 26, 2, 2, 3, 6 ). In some cases 
these marks are fairly deep ; in others superficial. The greater number of the 
grip'-marked stones are of a convenient shape for grasping with the hand and then 



190 OUDHEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG, DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 


the marks are in good places to afford a hold to the thumb and index finger. This 
is the reason why we call them „grip-marked'' These stones nearly always show signs 
of use at the edges. It must be noted, however, that some specimens with grip-marks 
are too heavy and unweildly to be used in the hand (PL 26, s). As far as we can 
see, on most of the stones, the marks were produced by pounding, but some of the 
cavities are so smooth that they must have been made by grinding, either from the 
start, or after preliminary pounding. Although we cannot prove it, we believe that 
the bigger ones were used as anvils, and the smaller ones as hammers or grinding 
stones, the marks, in the latter case, perhaps (though we are not sure of it) being 
made on purpose to give the hand a firm grip. The bruises occasioned by pounding, 
and the smooth surfaces by grinding, show us to what special uses the stones have 
been put. From this it is quite clear that both grip -marked pebbles, and others 
without marks, were utilized for both purposes, and we even got one or two examples 
in which one edge has been used for grinding and another for pounding. Grinding 
and pounding stones occur throughout the whole deposits. 

In close relation to the grinding-stones must be mentioned the grinding-slabs, 
also found in all the layers. {PL 26, */; 27, r/). These are mostly larger stones 
with one flat surface, while their under sides rest easily on the ground. On the 
flat side they are more or less hollowed by constant use. Two of the grinding-slabs 
that we encountered, seemingly of a sort of micaceous schist, must be specially 
noted, as both of them are hollowed on either side by grinding, and each has a 
hole in the centre in the thinnest part {PL 27, 2 ). It is obvious that the holes were 
made on purpose. We are unable to give a definite explanation of the exact use 
of these curious pieces. It may be that a string was passed through the holes and 
they were worn as amulets, although it appears that they are far too heavy for 
that use. If we could be sure that the cave-dwellers made use of wind-shelters, 
sleeping platforms, etc., made from branches, it might be possible that the holes were 
made for suspending the slabs in a handy position. A third solution is that the 
holes may have been made with a view to allowing material, already ground, to 
fall below. In the two last cases, however, pieces of considerable size must have 
passed through if the stone was used as a grinding slab ^). In layer D we encountered 
four pieces of stone with long, narrow and deep troughs in them ^ occasioned by 
grinding {PL 27, 1 , j). Two of these stones have a trough on one side; the other 
two one on both sides. Although we did not meet implements of the same curves as 
the troughs, according to finds in other countries, we do not hesitate to call them 
polishing slabs. 

Several of the grinding slabs are very distinctly coloured with a red pigment

ruddle and haematite were found. In layers A and B whole pockets of ruddle 
were encountered, while layers C, D and E also gave a good quantity, and not 
a single layer was entirely barren. Some of the pieces showed surfaces worn and 
flattened by rubbing {PL te 28 » 1, 2). Primitive man's custom of painting his body, 
in most cases with red paint exactly similar to ours, is too well known to need 
further comment on our finds. The paint, ground on the slabs, was probably mixed 
with marrow or animal fat. Other bodily ornament was scarcely used, as far as 
we can conclude from the results of our excavation. 

We have already noted a piece of Nautilus shell that may have been used 
as an ornament, similarly a squared piece of V^oluta shell. Two other shell objects 
have certainly been worn. One is part of a Pinna shell, with a hole bored at 
the point, found in layer A. {PL 28, 4), We believe that we are even able to 
conclude that it was used for a long time, as the part of the shell nearest to the 
body, when it was worn on a string, is much polished. Of more interest still is 
part of a money cowry {Cypraea) from layer B. {PL 28, i a-b). The whole of the 
dorsal surface has been ground away, so that only a flat piece, perforated by the 
silt of the lips, remains. As already noted, exactly similar objects have been 
excavated in Indo-China. The only other specimen which is certainly an ornament 
is a canine tooth bored for suspension tovsrards the root {PL 28, s). 

Pottery of various hinds was found in the upper layers. The roughest piece 
of all was, curiously enough, found in layer A. The greater part of the wares 
were either plain {PL 29, 9 ) or cord-marked {PL 29, 2 , 4, s, 6), but a few fragments 
of some vessels decorated with other ornamentation were also encountered. First 
of all, sherds ornamented with parallel, wavy lines came from layers A and 
B {PL 29, 5, 7), and a small piece of ware with slight incisions from layer 
A. {PL 29, i). The most remarkable pattern is on a sherd from layer E, {PL 29, 
The surface of this is divided by horizontal and vertical bars into small sunken 
squares ^), We cannot explain how this pattern was made, but think it possible 
that the squares may have been produced by pressing a stamp on the wet clay. 
As we have already said, by far the most of the pottery is cord-marked, but two 
varieties of this can be distinguished. In one the cord has been applied so as to 
form diamond-shaped reticulations, {PL 29, 4, 5 ), while in the other it has been 
used to produce more or less parallel lines {PL 29, 2 , 

Some small sherds of Chinese glazed ware were found. With one exception, 
these came from near the surface. This one piece occurred at 3.29 metres. If Chinese 
glazed ware was used at the time that the layers of this depth were formed, we 
certainly should have found more of it, and, as the deposits are full of holes of 
burrowing animals, we consider that it was certainly brought down from the surface 
by their action. 


Probably this pattern is an imitation of basket-work. 



1^2 OUDHEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG, DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 


In ending our summary of the specimens found, we must mention small 
fragments of a thin vessel of some white metal (tin? silver?) and three pieces of 
iron, one being a complete knife blade of modem Malayan type, the second pro- 
bably part of a similar weapon, and the third an indeterminate part of an iron 
tool. The bits of the metal vessel and the two first pieces of iron were found 
right outside the shelter, at the far end of the slope and quite near the surface. 
The third piece of iron came from further inside. 

As we have pointed out already, at the time the shell bank was formed the 
slope was much steeper than it is now. The basal curve of the shell bank proves 
that the implement was lying beyond the slope at the time the upper layers were 
formed. So, even if we do not ascribe the burying of this piece of iron to animal 
agency, it must, in any case, be of rather late date. 

As the deposits at Gunung Pondok are the first of great depth which have 
yet been methodically excavated in the Peninsula, it is dangerous to draw very 
definite conclusions from an examination of them. However, we will set down 
some facts that seem to us to be of interest. First of all, we are convinced that 
no great value can be attached to the objects found in the slope and that, for 
safety 's sake, it is better to consider only results obtained in the shelter proper, 
the part A, B, C, D in the maps. 

The followings are the facts referred to above: 

1) . That palaeoliths, Sumatra-type implements and proto-neoliths are asso- 
ciated throughout the deposits. 

2) . That cord-marked and pl^lin pottery both belong to the palaeo-protoneo- 
lithic stage of culture, but only to its later phases, as no cord-marked pottery, and 
only some sherds of plain pottery, were found in the lower layers— these, perhaps, 
having been brought down by animals. 

3, That red paint, for painting the body, was used throughout the whole 
period. 

4 ). That the same was the case with grinding-stones, pounding-stones and 
grinding-slabs . 

6). That it seems from the shape of the protoneoliths that their makers had 
the older types of the true neolithic culture as examples. 

6) . That a palaeolithic civilisation, making use of Sumatra- type implements, 
probably spread at a certain period through the south eastern parts of the conti- 
nent of Asia and even to Sumatra. 

7) . That the palaeo-protoneolithic stage (the Bacsonian of the French) also 
spread over the same area but, as far as we know at present, did not reach 
Sumatra. 


It is quite possible that the art of making pottciy was imported by the same people 
which accordUng to the French prehistorians brought the art of polishing to south-eastern Asia.

Madjapahit afkomstig zijn; het zal zich nu ergens in Engeland bevinden. Veel is er 
niet aan verloren, aangezien, hoezeer ook de verschillende Durga 's verse hillen in de

bekend heeft gemaakt. De passage, waarin ^Vallace spreekt over de wijze, waarop

verbazing van den schrijver over de vlug- en goedgeefsheid van den ,,W^aidono na 
een printah van z^n begeleider wel wat naief moet genoemd worden.

ruines op het Madjapahit-terrein ; waar toenmaals in deze omgeving wel geen suiker

medegedeeld, koffie, ligt het voor de hand de opbloei van de suikercultures ook hier 
aansprakelijk te stellen voor het totaal verdwgnen dezer talr^ke sporen. Ook is er 
van de plaveisel-sporen niet zoo veel meer te zien als Wallace doet voorkomen. De 
besproken poort is natuurlijk ^^ringin Lawang. Van Brahoe, Badjang Ratoe enz. 
schijnt hi) niets gezien te hebben.

Pag. 77. "'I stopped at Modjo-kerto, . 'The day after my arrival, Mr. 

Ball drove me over to the village of Modjo-Agong, where he was building a house 
and premises for the tobacco trade, which is carried on here by a system of native 
cultivation and advance purchase, somewhat similar to the indigo trade in British 
India. On our way we stayed to look at a fragment of the ruins of the ancient 
city of Modjo-pahit, consisting of two lofty brick masses, apparently the sides of 
a gateway. The extreme perfection and the beauty of the brickwork astonished me. 
The bricks are exceedingly fine and hard, with sharp ctngles and true surfaces. They 
are laid with great exactness, without visible mortar or cement, yet somehow fast- 
ened together so that the joints are hardly perceptible, and sometimes the two 
surfaces coalesce in a most incomprehensible manner. Such admirable brickwork I 
have never seen before or since. There was no sculpture here, but abundance of 
bold projections and finely- worked mouldings. Traces of buildings exist for many 
miles in every direction, and almost every road and pathway shows a foundation 
of brickwork beneath it — the paved roads of the old city. In the house of the 
Waidono or district chief at Modjo-agong, I saw a beautiful figure carved in heigh 
relief out of a block of lava, and which had been found burried in the ground near 
the village. On my expressing a wish to obtain some such specimen, Mr. B. asked 
the chief for it, and much to my surprise he immediately gave it me. It represented 
the Hindoo goddess Durga, called in Java, Lora Jonggrang (the exalted virgin). She 
has eight arms, and stands on the back of a kneeling bull. Her lower right hand 
holds the tail of the bull, while the corresponding left hand grasps the hair of a captive, 
Dewth Mahikusor, the personification of vice, who has attempted to slay her bull. 
He has a cord round his waist, and crouches at her feet in an attitude of supplication. 
The other hands of the goddess hold, on her right side, a double hook or small 

anchor, a broad straight sword, and a noose of thick cord; on her left, a girdle 

or armlet of large beads or (78) shells, an unstrung bow, and a standard or war 

flag. This deity was a special favourite among the old Javanese, and her image is 

often found in the ruined temples which abound in the eastern part of the island. 

The specimen I had obtained was a small one, about two feet high, weighing 
perhaps a himdredweight; and the next day we had it conveyed to Modjo-kerto to 
await my return to Sourabaya.*^ 

Pag. 79. "The road to Wonosalem led through a magnificent forest, in the 
depths of which we passed a fine ruin of what appeared to have been a royal tomb 
or mausoleum. It is formed entirely of stone, and elaborately carved. Near the base 
is a course of boldly projecting blocks, sculptured in high rehef, with a series of 
scenes which are probably incidents in the life of the defunct. These are al 




OUDHEIDKUNDIG VERSLAG, DERDE EN VIERDE KWARTAAL 1926 197 


beautifully executed, some of the figures of animals in particular being easely 
recognisable and very accurate. The general design, as far as the ruined state of 
the upper part will permit of its being seen, is very good, effect being given by 
an immense number and variety of projecting or retreating courses of squared stones 
in place of mouldings. The size of this structure is about thirty feet square by 
twenty high, and as the traveller comes suddenly upon it on a small elevation on 
the road side, overshadowed by gigantic trees, overrun with plants and creepers, 
and closel^^ bached by the gloomy forest, he is struck ( 80 ) by the solemnity and 
picturesque beauty of the scene, and is led to ponder on the strange law of pro- 
gress, which looks so like retrogression, and which in so many distant parts of the 
world has exterminated or driven out a highly artistic and constructive race, to make 
room for one which, as far as we can judge, is very far its inferior. 

Few Englishmen are aware of the number and beauty of the architec- 
tural remains in Java. They have never been popularly illustrated or described, and 
it will therefore take most persons by surprise to learn that they far surpass those 
of Central America, perhaps even those of India. To give some idea of these ruins, 
and perchance to excite wealthy amateurs to explore them thoroughly and obtain 
by photography an accurate record of their beautiful sculptures before it is to late, 
I will enumerate the most important, as briefly described in Sir Stamford Raffles 
History of Java”. 

Voigt een exerpt uit bovengenoemd boek over de tempels van Brambanam 
Borobodo, Gunong Prau, doorspekt met opmerkingen, die echter aangezien ^Vallace 
deze tempels nimmer aanschouwd heeft, van geen beteekenis zijn. 

Dat Wallace van een mausoleum spreekt is tamelijk merkwaardig; inderdaad 
is Tjandi Ngrimbi er een en wel waarschijnhjk van de hoofdvrouw van den stichter 
van Madjapahit. Het volksverhaal spreekt van een patapan en kan dus van geen invloed 
op zijn meening zijn geweest; de begraafplaats van Dewi Arimbi ligt in de sawahs, 
volgens de omwonenden. 

Merkwaardig ook is zijn jammerklacht over de degeneratie van het javaansche 
volk, te merkwaardig er omdat deze geuit is bij het aanschouwen van een oost- 
javaansch bouwwerk, terwijl anderen deze klacht plegen te uiten als zij een 
middenjavaansch bouwsel te zien krijgen. Dat schijnt echter voor een goed laudator 
temporis acti van geen bijzondere beteekenis te zijn en wij mogen van een passant 
als Wallace zeker niet verwachten dat hij zich emstig rekenschap zou hebben gegeven 
aangaande de oorzaken van het verdw^nen van dergelijke tjandi s. Zijn tijd ver 
vooruit was hij ongetwijfeld in zooverre hij niet spreekt van Hindoes, die deze 
tempels zouden gebouwd hebben maar de oude Javanen als de bouwers beschouwde.
